Traveling waves near Couette Flow for the 2D Euler equation
Ángel Castro, Daniel Lear

Abstract
. In this paper we reveal the existence of a large family of new, nontrivial and smooth traveling waves for the 2D Euler equation at an arbitrarily small distance from the Couette flow in H s H^{s} , with s < 3 / 2 s<3/2 , at the level of the vorticity. The speed of these waves is of order 1 with respect to this distance. This result strongly contrasts with the setting of very high regularity in Gevrey spaces (see [ 7 ] ), where the problem exhibits an inviscid damping mechanism that leads to relaxation of perturbations back to nearby shear flows. It also complements the fact that there not exist nontrivial traveling waves in the H 3 2 + H^{\frac{3}{2}+} neighborhoods of Couette flow (see [ 51 ] ).



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
76E05,76B03,35Q31,35Q35

1.
Introduction and main result

In this paper we deal with the incompressible Euler equations in the two-dimensional
space
D:=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. In the vorticity formulation 2D Euler is given by the transport equation

(1)

({\bf x},t)\in D\times\mathbb{R}^{+},\qquad\left\{\begin{split}&\partial_{t}w+{\bf v}\cdot\nabla w=0,\\
&{\bf v}=\nabla^{\perp}\Delta^{-1}w,\\
&w|_{t=0}=w_{0},\end{split}\right.

where
{\bf v}=(v_{1},v_{2})
denotes the velocity field and
w=\partial_{x}v_{2}-\partial_{y}v_{1}
its vorticity. The starting point of this paper is the fact that the Couette flow, given by
{\bf v}=(y,0)
and
w=-1
, is a steady state for (
1
). We are interested in perturbations of the form
{\bf v}({\bf x},t):=(y,0)+{\bf u}({\bf x},t)
with total vorticity
w({\bf x},t):=-1+\omega({\bf x},t),
where
\omega=\text{curl}({\bf u}).
The equations for a perturbation around the Couette flow are given by

(2)

\left\{\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\omega+y\partial_{x}\omega+{\bf u}\cdot\nabla\omega=0,\\
&{\bf u}=\nabla^{\perp}\Delta^{-1}\omega,\\
&\omega|_{t=0}=\omega_{0}.\end{split}\right.

Here, the operator
\Delta^{-1}
is given by expression (
6
).

Stability of
shear flows
, to which the Couette flow belongs, is a widely investigated problem in the context of hydrodynamic stability, a field where many questions still remain unanswered. We refer to
[
8
,
30
]
for an overview of this topic and a detailed list of references.

The Couette flow is one of the simplest shear flows if not the simplest one, however, it poses several long-standing puzzles in hydrodynamics.
The issue that Couette flow is known to be spectrally stable for all Reynolds
numbers in contradiction with instabilities observed in experiments is now often referred as the

Sommerfeld paradox
or
turbulence paradox
. There have been many attempts in the literature to find an explanation to this paradox starting in the nineteenth century with Stokes, Helmholtz, Reynolds, Rayleigh, Kelvin, Orr, Sommerfeld and many others.

The linear analysis for the Couette flow has been obtained via an eigenvalue analysis, however, the classical stability analysis in general does not agree with the numerical and physical observations. A key observation was made by Trefethen et al.
[
60
]
, where it has been shown that a pure eigenvalues analysis could hide several problems. These problems are due to the fact that the operators involved in the linearization around a shear flow are in general non-normal. Nowadays, the idea that the interaction between nonlinear effects and non-normal transient growth can lead to instabilities is classical in fluid mechanics, see
[
59
,
60
]
. We refer to
[
50
]
and the references therein for a modern approach and a mathematical treatment to the problem.

There were many attempts to find an explanation to the Sommerfeld paradox. One of the first attempt might be due to Orr, who studied the linear
stability directly by considering the initial value problem. In this case, the problem reduces to
a simple transport equation for the vorticity. By recovering the velocity via the Biot-Savart law, Orr observed that the velocity may experience a transient growth, suggesting that this phenomenon may be a possible source of instabilities in the nonlinear problem. In fact, one can see assuming enough regularity on the initial perturbation that the vertical velocity tends to zero when times goes to infinity. This convergence back to equilibrium, despite time reversibility and the lack of dissipative mechanisms, is now referred as
inviscid damping
.
This phenomenon share analogies with the
Landau damping
in the kinetic theory of plasma physics, see the paper of Mohout–Villani
[
57
]
and references therein.

Despite the understanding of the linear setting, the nonlinear problem is substantially harder and remained unresolved until the breakthrough of Bedrossian–Masmoudi
[
7
]
. They established that sufficiently small perturbations in the Gevrey spaces
\mathcal{G}^{s}
, with
s>1/2
, converge strongly in
L^{2}
(of velocity) to a shear flow near Couette as times goes to infinity in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
.

As it was shown in
[
22
]
by Deng–Masmoudi, the Gevrey regularity requirement
s>1/2
is crucial. Indeed, Deng and Masmoudi prove long time instabilities in
\mathcal{G}^{1/2^{-}}
.

In very recent works Ionescu–Jia
[
44
,
45
]
and Masmoudi–Zhao
[
56
]
proved that nonlinear asymptotic stability holds true also for perturbations around the Couette flow and more general monotonic shear flows, with compactly supported vorticity, in the periodic channel
\mathbb{T}\times[-1,1].

Motivated by these results, the linearized equations around more general shear flows solutions were
investigated intensely in the last few years, and linear inviscid damping and decay was proven
in many cases of physical interest, see for example
[
6
,
11
,
20
,
23
,
24
,
38
,
48
,
49
,
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
]
.
For the analogous problem in the viscous setting or even in the compressible case, we refer to
[
5
,
3
,
4
,
19
,
53
,
18
,
1
]
for a comprehensive but not exhaustive list of references. Similar analysis have been also carried out recently for the Boussinesq system, see for example
[
9
,
10
,
25
,
55
,
66
,
67
]
.

For the most up-to-date overview of this subject, see
[
8
]
and references therein.

By all the above, an important direction to explore further is the role of nonlinear instabilities, and moreover, how the regularity of initial data may or may not affect the dynamics.

Nonlinear long time dynamics near Couette flow is related to the existence of nontrivial invariant structures as steady-states, traveling waves, etc. Non existence of nontrivial invariant structures near Couette flow is necessary for nonlinear inviscid damping. Conversely, their existence means nonlinear inviscid damping is not true, and long time dynamics near Couette flow may be richer.

In this direction we refer to the work of Lin–Zeng
[
51
]
, where the authors construct nontrivial steady flows arbitrarily close to Couette flow in
H^{s}
, with
s<3/2
, in a finite channel
\mathbb{T}\times[-1,1]
. In addition, the non existence of nontrivial traveling waves close to the Couette in
H^{s}
, with
s>3/2
, is proved in this paper. Here and in the rest of the paper “trivial” means independent of
x
.

It is important to emphasize that the steady cat’s eyes structure near
Couette flow, constructed in the aforementioned paper
[
51
]
, can be used to obtain traveling waves using the symmetries of the system (
1
).
Note that, by Galilean invariance, if
{\bf v}({\bf x},t)
solves 2D Euler equation then
\overline{{\bf v}}({\bf x},t):={\bf v}(x+\lambda t,y,t)-(\lambda,0)
also solves it. Therefore, if
{\bf v}({\bf x})
is a steady solution from
[
51
]
, we get that
\overline{{\bf v}}({\bf x},t):={\bf v}(x+\lambda t,y)-(\lambda,0)
is a traveling wave solution of 2D Euler.

Since steady solutions in
[
51
]
satisfy
v_{1}({\bf x})=y+O(\epsilon)
, where
\epsilon
measure the distance to the Couette flow, the size of
\overline{v}_{1}-y
will be
\lambda
. So, in order to obtain a traveling wave
\epsilon-
close to the Couette flow, the speed of the wave must be of the same order, i.e.,
\lambda=O(\epsilon).

In the present paper we are concerned with the existence of nontrivial and smooth traveling waves close to Couette flow in the
H^{3/2^{-}}
topology with speed
\lambda
of order 1 and with
v_{1}=y+O(\epsilon)
. Then our solutions are of a different nature that those of
[
51
]
.

It is necessary to mention here also the result of Li–Lin
[
50
]
, where the authors prove the existence of traveling waves bifurcating from the sequence,
v_{n}(y):=y+\frac{A}{n}\sin(4n\pi y)
for
\frac{1}{8\pi}<A<\frac{1}{4\pi}.

As
n\to\infty
, the oscillatory shears approach the Couette flow, i.e.,
v_{n}(y)\to y
in
L^{2}

and
L^{\infty}
. On the other hand, in the vorticity variable, the oscillatory shears do not
approach the Couette since
\partial_{y}v_{n}(y)=1+4A\pi\cos(4n\pi y)\not\to 1
in any Lebesgue norm.

Recently, there has been a growing interest in the study of existence or not of invariant structures as steady-states and traveling waves for the incompressible 2D Euler equations near other shear flows and for related equations. For example, we refer to
[
52
,
54
]
for the 2D Euler equation with Coriolis force or
[
19
]
for the case of Kolmogorov and Poiseuille flows.

For the sake of clarity we shall now give an elementary statement of our main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any
0\leq s<3/2
and
\epsilon>0
, the perturbed 2D Euler system (
2
) admits a nontrivial traveling wave solution that satisfies the smallness condition

\|w+1\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})}\equiv\|\omega\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})}<\epsilon,

with
\omega\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
compactly supported, and whose speed is
O(1)
with respect to
\epsilon
.

This result will be the consequence of Theorems
6.1
,
6.2
and
6.4
where one can get a deeper insight on the properties of these solutions.

An important tool behind the proof will be the bifucartion theory, which has been very useful to prove the existence of solutions with a global structure in several equations arising in the field of fluid mechanics. Following the approach of Burbea
[
12
]
, there has been several works concerning the existence of single or multiple patches
moving without changing shape, not only for 2D Euler equation but also for the generalized Surface Quasi-Geostrophic equations (SQG
)_{\beta}
where
0<\beta<2
. We refer to
[
13
,
14
,
40
,
43
]
for single rotating patches,
[
26
,
27
]
for doubly connected
V-states,
[
42
,
39
]
for corotating and counter-rotating vortex pairs and
[
33
]
for steady states. See
[
34
]
for further properties of rotating solutions and
[
35
,
36
]
for the case of the vortex-sheet problem. See
[
29
,
41
,
58
]
for related constructions.

The existence of smooth rotating vortices is more intricate due to the higher dimension of the space on which the linear part of the equation acts. In spite of this, in
[
15
,
16
]
smooth rotating solutions were constructed for SGQ equation and for 2D Euler equation, respectively. See also
[
2
]
for the construction of a different type of smooth rotating solutions for SQG and
[
37
]
for the existence of traveling waves also for SQG. The existence of non-smooth rotating vortices with non-uniform densities can be found in
[
32
]
for 2D Euler.

In a broad sense, these results connect with that developed in
[
6
]
, where the authors analyze the incompressible 2D Euler equation linearized around a radially symmetric, strictly monotone decreasing vorticity distribution. For sufficiently regular data some interesting phenomena appear, such as
vortex axysimmetrization
(the vorticity weakly converges back to radial symmetry) and
vorticity depletion
(faster inviscid damping rates than those possible with passive scalar evolution). Finally, for perturbations around coherent vortex structures we also refer to
[
20
,
31
,
46
,
47
,
61
]
and references therein.

1.1.
Sketch of the proof

We will assume that the level sets of the vorticity are given by the family of graphs
(x,f({\bf x},t))
, i.e.,

\omega(x,f({\bf x},t),t)=\varpi(y).

Then, all the information of the problem is encoded in the time-evolution of level curves
f({\bf x},t)
and in the profile function
\varpi.
After some algebraic manipulations the problem reduces to the equation:

(3)

\frac{\mbox{d\,\,}}{\mbox{d}t}f({\bf x},t)+f({\bf x},t)\partial_{x}f({\bf x},t)=\mathbf{U}_{\varpi}[f]({\bf x},t),\qquad\text{for }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime}),

where
\mathbf{U}_{\varpi}[\cdot]
is a nonlocal and nonlinear functional defined below in (
13
).

In order to look for traveling wave solutions we will take

f({\bf x},t):=y+\mathsf{f}(x+\lambda t,y)

which will lead to an equation for
(\lambda,\mathsf{f}({\bf x}))
of the form,

(4)

F_{\varpi}[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})=0,\qquad\text{for }{\bf x}\in\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime}).

with
F_{\varpi}[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]
given in (
17
). This equation we will be solved by using the Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem (C-R). Note that
F_{\varpi}[\lambda,0]=0
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and one can expect to bifurcate from zero for some values of
\lambda
. We also be able to get enough information on
\mathsf{f}({\bf x})\equiv\mathsf{f}(x,y)
to guarantee that it depend on the horizontal variable
x
in a nontrivial way.

The size of our solution
\omega
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
,
0<s<\frac{3}{2}
, which coincide with the distance of our solutions
w=-1+\omega
to the Couette flow, will be given by the size of the profile
\varpi
in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
. Thus, we have to found a small enough profile
\varpi
for which we can solve (
4
). A full description of
\varpi
will be given in Section
2.3
. At this point let us says that it will be a
\kappa-
regularization of the profile of size
\varepsilon
we see in Figure
1
:

Figure 1.
Profile
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\,0}
.

The core of the paper will be to study the spectral properties of the operator

\mathcal{L}[\lambda]:=D_{\mathsf{f}}F_{\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}}[\lambda,0].

This analysis will be made by an asymptotic analysis on
\varepsilon
.

1.2.
Organization

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we shall write the equation for the level curves of the vorticity through the Biot-Savart law. In Section
3
, we shall
introduce and review some background material on the bifurcation theory and Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem.
In Section
4
, we will define the spaces we will work with in order to apply the C-R theorem and study the regularity of the nonlinear functional. In Section
5
, we conduct the spectral study and check that our equation satisfies the hypotheses of the C-R theorem. In Section
6.1
, we obtain quantitative bounds for the
H^{s}
-norm in terms of all the parameters involved in the problem. We address the full regularity of the traveling wave solution in Section
6.2
. Finally, in the last section, in order to facilitate the presentation, we collect in the Appendix the proofs of all technical lemmas used in the paper.

2.
Formulation of the problem

In this section it will be obtained an equation whose solutions yield traveling waves of (
2
).

2.1.
The 2D Euler as an equation for the level curves of the vorticity

We will obtain the equation for the level curves of the vorticity of a solution of the 2D Euler equation (
2
). We will use the stream function
\psi
, which satisfies

(5)

{\bf u}=\nabla^{\perp}\psi,\qquad\Delta\psi=\omega.

The Green function for the Laplacian in the strip
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
is given (see
[
17
]
) by the convolution with the kernel

K(\bar{{\bf x}})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log[\cosh(\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x})].

Consequently, the stream function
\psi=K\ast\omega
is given by the expression

(6)

\psi({\bf x},t)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\log\left(\cosh\left(y-\bar{y}\right)-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right)\omega(\bar{{\bf x}},t)d\bar{{\bf x}}.

We will find solutions of (
2
) by looking to the level curves of
\omega
. Assuming that these level curves can be parameterized by the family of graphs
(x,\,f({\bf x},t))
, with
\partial_{y}f({\bf x},t)>0
, we have that

(7)

\displaystyle\omega(x,f({\bf x},t),t)=\varpi(y),

for some smooth and even profile function
\varpi
. We remark that equation (
2
) is a transport type equation, then the profile
\varpi
can be taken independent on
t
without any loss of generality.

By straightforward computations, we get

(8)

\nabla\omega(x,f({\bf x},t),t)=\frac{\varpi^{\prime}(y)}{\partial_{y}f({\bf x},t)}(-\partial_{x}f({\bf x},t),1).

It is easily seen that taking a time derivative on (
7
) and using (
2
) yields

(9)

\partial_{t}f({\bf x},t)\partial_{y}\omega(x,f({\bf x},t),t)=f({\bf x},t)\partial_{x}\omega(x,f({\bf x},t),t)+{\bf u}(x,f({\bf x},t),t)\cdot\nabla\omega(x,f({\bf x},t),t).

Plugging (
8
) into (
9
) we obtain

(10)

\left[\partial_{t}f({\bf x},t)+f({\bf x},t)\partial_{x}f({\bf x},t)-\mathbf{U}[f]({\bf x},t)\right]\frac{\varpi^{\prime}(y)}{\partial_{y}f({\bf x},t)}=0,

where

(11)

\mathbf{U}[f]({\bf x},t):={\bf u}(x,f({\bf x},t),t)\cdot(-\partial_{x}f({\bf x},t),1).

Next we obtain an expression for
\mathbf{U}[f]({\bf x},t)
. Taking
\nabla^{\perp}
on (
6
) we have that

\displaystyle{\bf u}({\bf x},t)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}}\log\left(\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right)\nabla^{\perp}\omega(\bar{{\bf x}},t)\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\supp(\nabla^{\perp}\omega)}\log\left(\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right)\nabla^{\perp}\omega(\bar{{\bf x}},t)\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}.

Making the change of variable
\bar{{\bf x}}=(\tilde{x},f(\tilde{{\bf x}},t))
and using expression (
8
) we have that

(12)

\displaystyle{\bf u}(x,y,t)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime})}\log\left(\cosh(y-f(\tilde{{\bf x}},t))-\cos(x-\tilde{x})\right)(1,\partial_{x}f(\tilde{{\bf x}},t))\varpi^{\prime}(\tilde{y})\mbox{d}\tilde{{\bf x}},

and

\displaystyle{\bf u}(x,f({\bf x},t),t)=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime})}\log\left(\cosh(f({\bf x},t)-f(\tilde{{\bf x}},t))-\cos(x-\tilde{x})\right)(1,\partial_{x}f(\tilde{{\bf x}},t))\varpi^{\prime}(\tilde{y})\mbox{d}\tilde{{\bf x}}.

Thus

(13)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}[f]({\bf x},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime})}\log\left(\cosh(f({\bf x},t)-f(\bar{{\bf x}},t))-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right)(\partial_{x}f({\bf x},t)-\partial_{1}f(\bar{{\bf x}},t))\varpi^{\prime}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime}(y-\cdot))}\varpi^{\prime}(y-\bar{y})\log\left(\cosh(\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[f]({\bf x},t))-\cos(\bar{x})\right)\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}f]({\bf x},t)\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}},

where in the sequel we shall use the notation

\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[f]({\bf x},t):=f({\bf x},t)-f({\bf x}-\bar{{\bf x}},t).

Since equation (
10
) is multiplied by
\varpi^{\prime}(y)
and in order to compute
\mathbf{U}[f]({\bf x},t)
in
\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime})
one just needs to know
f({\bf x},t)
in
\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime}),
we have to solve

(14)

\partial_{t}f({\bf x},t)+f({\bf x},t)\partial_{x}f({\bf x},t)=\mathbf{U}[f]({\bf x},t),\qquad{\bf x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}\times\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{\supp}}(\varpi^{\prime}).

Conversely, if
f(\cdot,t)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime}))
, with
\partial_{y}f>0
, satisfies the previous equation (
14
) on
\mathbb{T}\times\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{\supp}}(\varpi^{\prime})
for some
\varpi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, we can prove that the function
\omega(\cdot,t)
defined by

\displaystyle\omega(x,f({\bf x},t),t)=\varpi^{\prime}(y)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime}),

and extended by suitable constants to the complementary of

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,(x,y)=(\bar{x},f(\bar{{\bf x}},t))\quad\text{with}\quad(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime})\}

is a smooth solution of the original problem (
2
) on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
.

2.2.
The equation for the traveling wave

We will look for solutions of the form

(15)

f({\bf x},t)=y+\mathsf{f}(x+\lambda t,y).

Putting the above ansatz (
15
) into (
14
) our problem reduces to solve the time independent equation

\lambda\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})+(y+\mathsf{f}({\bf x}))\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})=\mathbf{U}[y+\mathsf{f}]({\bf x}),

or equivalently

(16)

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})=0,\qquad{\bf x}\in\mathbb{T}\times\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\circ}}{{\supp}}(\varpi^{\prime}),

where

(17)

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x}):=\lambda\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})+(y+\mathsf{f}({\bf x}))\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})\\
-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\supp(\varpi^{\prime})}\varpi^{\prime}(\bar{y})\log\left[\cosh(y-\bar{y}+\mathsf{f}({\bf x})-\mathsf{f}(\bar{{\bf x}}))-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right](\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})-\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}(\bar{{\bf x}}))\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}},

It is easy to check that
\mathsf{f}({\bf x})=0
is a trivial solution of (
16
) whose velocity and vorticiy are given by
{\bf u}({\bf x},t)=(u_{1}(y),0)
and
\omega({\bf x},t)=-\partial_{y}u_{1}(y)
where
-\partial_{y}u_{1}(y)=\varpi(y)
. Consequently,
f({\bf x},t)=y
is a solution of (
14
) whose velocity is a degenerate shear flow. To prove our goal, we will need to show the existence of nontrivial solutions of the equation (
14
).
More specifically, we will prove the existence of traveling wave solutions of (
14
) bifurcating from the trivial one
f({\bf x},t)=y
.

Remark 2.1
.

The solutions given by
\omega(x,y+\mathsf{f}(x+\lambda t,t)=\varpi(y)
give rise, by expression (
12
) and an appropriate change of variables, to solutions of 2D Euler

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bf u}+y\partial_{x}{\bf u}+({\bf u}\cdot\nabla){\bf u}+\nabla p=0,\quad\text{in $\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}$,}

of the form

{\bf u}({\bf x},t)=(u_{1}(x,y,t),u_{2}(x,y,t))=(\bar{u}_{1}(x+\lambda t,y),\bar{u}_{2}(x+\lambda t,y)).

2.3.
The profile function
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
.

To continue, we define the profile function
\varpi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
that will be used in Section
5
to solve the functional equation (
16
).

To start with let us construct first a profile
\varpi\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{\frac{3}{2}^{-}}(\mathbb{R})
. This profile will be even and compactly supported. Let us consider the auxiliary function

\displaystyle\varphi(z)=\frac{1-z}{2},\quad z\in[-1,1].

Let
\varepsilon>0,
we define
\varpi_{\varepsilon}(y)
in the following way

\displaystyle\varpi_{\varepsilon}(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cr}0&y>1+\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon\varphi\left(\frac{y-1}{\varepsilon}\right)&1-\varepsilon\leq y\leq 1+\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon&0<y<1-\varepsilon.\end{array}\right.

Notice that
\varpi_{\varepsilon}(y)
is extended as an even function for
y<0
.

Since we are interested in smooth solutions, we need to consider a smooth profile function
\varpi
. We will use a
\kappa
-regularization of
\varphi
, note that
\varphi^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}
. The function
\varphi_{\kappa}
will be defined by

\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(z)=-\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(z)}{\int_{-1}^{1}\psi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}},\quad\text{for }|z|<1,

with

\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(z)=\int_{-1}^{1}\chi_{[-1+\kappa,1-\kappa]}(\bar{z})\Theta_{\kappa}(z-\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}=\int_{-1+\kappa}^{1-\kappa}\Theta_{\kappa}(z-\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z},

where
\Theta
is a mollifier, i.e a smooth, positive function, with
\supp(\Theta)\subset(-1,1)
and
\int_{-1}^{1}\Theta(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}=1
. Then, for each
\kappa>0
we define

\Theta_{\kappa}(z)=\frac{1}{\kappa}\Theta\left(\frac{z}{\kappa}\right).

Notice that, since
\Theta
is smooth then
\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}
is also smooth. Moreover, since
\supp(\Theta_{\kappa})\subset(-\kappa,\kappa)
we have that
\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(z)=0
for
|z|>1
and it is easy to check that

\psi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(z)=1
for
-1+2\kappa<z<1-2\kappa
. Then,
\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}\to 1
as
\kappa\to 0
. This convergence holds in
L^{1}([-1,1])
and actually
\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}\to\chi_{[-1,1]}
in
H^{\frac{3}{2}-}(\mathbb{R})
. Furthermore, one can check that

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1\right|\mbox{d}\bar{z}=\int_{-1}^{-1+2\kappa}\left|\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1\right|\mbox{d}\bar{z}+\int_{1-2\kappa}^{1}\left|\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1\right|\mbox{d}\bar{z}\leq C\kappa,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-1}^{1}\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}-2\right|=\left|\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1\right)\mbox{d}\bar{z}\right|\leq\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1\right|\mbox{d}\bar{z}\leq C\kappa,

and then

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\left|\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})}{\int_{-1}^{1}\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}}-\frac{1}{2}\right|\mbox{d}\bar{z}=\frac{1}{\left|2\int_{-1}^{1}\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}\right|}\int_{-1}^{1}\left|2\left(\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1\right)+\left(2-\int_{-1}^{1}\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}\right)\right|\mbox{d}\bar{z}\leq C\kappa.

We will take

(18)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\kappa}(z)=1+\int_{-1}^{z}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}=1-\frac{\int_{-1}^{z}\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}}{\int_{-1}^{1}\psi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}}.

We summarize the properties of this function in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

The function
\varphi_{\kappa}
given by (
18
) satisfies

(1)

\varphi_{\kappa}(-1)=1
,
\varphi_{\kappa}(1)=0
,
(\partial_{z}^{n}\varphi_{\kappa})(\pm 1)=0
, for
n=1,2,\ldots

(2)

\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(z)<0
, for
|z|<1
.

(3)

\|\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}\|_{L^{1}([-1,1])}\leq C\kappa
.

The profile
\varpi
that will be used in this manuscript is given by

\displaystyle\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cr}0&y>1+\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon\varphi_{\kappa}\left(\frac{y-1}{\varepsilon}\right)&1-\varepsilon\leq y\leq 1+\varepsilon,\\
\varepsilon&0<y<1-\varepsilon.\end{array}\right.

Notice that
\varpi_{\varepsilon}(y)
is extended as an even function, i.e. for any
y<0
we take
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y)=\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(-y)
.
Let us point out that the support of
\omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
will be the domain

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}=[-1-\varepsilon,-1+\varepsilon]\cup[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon].

The rest of the paper consists in finding a nontrivial solution of (
16
) with
\varpi\equiv\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
in (
17
) for parameters
\varepsilon
and
\kappa
small enough. It will be done using the bifurcation theory through Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem
[
21
]
. For the completeness of the paper we recall this basic theorem and it will referred to as sometimes by C-R theorem.

3.
Bifurcation theory and Crandall-Rabinowitz

Before going into details, we shall first fix some notations that we will used later. For a linear mapping
\mathcal{L}
we will denote by
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L})
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{L})
the kernel and range of
\mathcal{L}
respectively. If
Y
is a vector space and
S
is a subspace, then
Y/S
denotes the quotient space.

Now, we intend to give some formal explanations and general picture of the bifurcation theory. This brief discussion will be closed by stating the details of the C-R theorem. Roughly speaking, the main objective of this theory is to look for the solutions of the functional equation

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]=0,

where
F:\mathbb{R}\times X\to Y
is a smooth continuous function between Banach spaces
X
and
Y
. We assume in addition
that
\mathsf{f}=0
is a trivial solution for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, that is,
F[\lambda,0]=0
. Whether close to the trivial solution
(\lambda_{\star},0)
one can find a branch of nontrivial ones is the main aim of this theory. If this is the case we say that there is a bifurcation at the point
(\lambda_{\star},0)
.
As the Implicit Function Theorem tells us, the first idea is to study the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}:=D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,0]:X\to Y
.
In principle, the involved Banach spaces
X
and
Y
are infinite-dimensional and thus the bifurcation analysis is in general complex. However, if the linearized operator around this point
(\lambda_{\star},0)
generates a Fredholm type operator, then one can use the so-called Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction in order to reduce the infinite-dimensional problem to a finite-dimensional one. Finally, for this last problem we just need some concrete transversal conditions so that the Implicit Function Theorem can be applied.

To sum up, this is the classical result proved by Crandall and Rabinowitz which is a basic tool in the bifurcation theory and that will be used in this manuscript. Now, we recall here the statement of this theorem from
[
21
]
for expository purposes.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
X,Y
be two Banach spaces,
V
a neighborhood of
0
in
X
and let

F:\mathbb{R}\times V\to Y

with the following properties:

(1)

F[\lambda,0]=0
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

The partial derivatives
D_{\lambda}F
,
D_{\mathsf{f}}F
and
D^{2}_{\lambda,\mathsf{f}}F
exist and are continuous.

(3)

There exists
\lambda_{\star}
such that if
\mathcal{L}_{\star}=D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda_{\star},0]
then
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
and
Y/\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
are one-dimensional.

(4)

Transversality assumption:
D^{2}_{\lambda,\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda_{\star},0]h_{\star}\not\in\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
, where

\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})=\text{span}\{h_{\star}\}.

If
Z
is any complement of
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{L}_{\star})
in
X
, then there is a neighborhood
U
of
(\lambda_{\star},0)
in
\mathbb{R}\times X
, an interval
(-\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})
, and continuous functions
\varphi:(-\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})\to\mathbb{R}
,
\psi:(-\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0})\to Z
such that
\varphi(0)=0
,
\psi(0)=0
and

\displaystyle F^{-1}(0)\cap U=

\displaystyle\Big\{\big(\lambda_{\star}+\varphi(\sigma),\sigma h_{\star}+\sigma\psi(\sigma)\big)\,;\,|\sigma|<\sigma_{0}\Big\}\cup\Big\{(\lambda,0)\,;\,(\lambda,0)\in U\Big\}.

The bulk of the paper consists in checking all the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
. This will be done in details in
the next sections.

4.
Functional setting and regularity

Note that our functional can be written as

(22)

F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})=\lambda\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})+(y+\mathsf{f}({\bf x}))\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\varpi^{\prime}(y-\bar{y})K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}},

where the kernel is given by the following expression

K[g]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}}):=\log\left[\cosh(\bar{y}+\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x}))-\cos(\bar{x})\right].

Recall that the finite difference is given by

\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x})=g({\bf x})-g({\bf x}-\bar{{\bf x}}),

and the domain of integration is just

D_{\varepsilon}(y)=\mathbb{T}\times I_{\varepsilon}(y),\quad\text{with}\quad I_{\varepsilon}(y)=y+I_{\varepsilon}.

For simplicity, we will use the notation
D_{\varepsilon}=D_{\varepsilon}(0)=\mathbb{T}\times I_{\varepsilon}.

Remark 4.1
.

In all this section we will assume that
\varpi\equiv\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
as in Section
2.3
.

4.1.
The functional setting

In order to apply C-R theorem we need first to fix the function spaces. We should look for Banach spaces
X
and
Y
such that
F:\mathbb{R}\times X\rightarrow Y
is well-defined and satisfies the required assumptions.

Our first step is to define the spaces we will work with in order to apply the Crandall-Rabinowitz theorem.
The spaces
X
and
Y
will be given by

(23)

\displaystyle X(D_{\varepsilon}):=\left\{g\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon}):g\text{ is even in $x$ with $\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}g(x,y)\,\mbox{d}x=0$}\right\},

and

(24)

\displaystyle Y(D_{\varepsilon}):=\left\{g\in H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon}):g\text{ is odd in $x$}\right\}.

Here
H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
is the Sobolev-Leibnitz space of
2\pi
-periocic functions in the
x
-variable with norm

\displaystyle\|g\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}:=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\sum_{j=0}^{3-i}\|\partial_{x}^{i}\partial_{y}^{j}g\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

After that the main purpose will be to prove next lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

For all
0<\varepsilon<1,
there exist
0<\delta(\varepsilon)\ll 1
small enough such that

\displaystyle F\,:\,\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))

\displaystyle\to Y(D_{\varepsilon}),

\displaystyle\quad(\lambda,\mathsf{f})

\displaystyle\to F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon})):=\{g\in X(D_{\varepsilon})\,:\,\|g\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}<\delta\}.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{f}\in X(D_{\varepsilon}),
the evenness in the
x
-direction translates into the oddness of
F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]
just by definition of the functional (
22
). Moreover, the non-integral part of the RHS of (
22
) maps
H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
into
H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})
trivially as a direct consequence of the algebraic property of the Sobolev space
H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})
. Therefore, we will focus our attention into the integral part of (
22
).
In addition, as the profile function
\varpi
is smooth, our functional is well-defined if each of the next terms are bounded as follow:

(25)

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{i}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3-i}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(\varepsilon,\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})})\qquad(0\leq i\leq 3).

Note that by definition of
\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x})
we have

(26)

\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}|\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x})|^{2}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right)\mbox{d}{\bf x}\leq 4|D_{\varepsilon}|\|g\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}.

For the case
i=0
or
i=1
, as
\mathsf{f}\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
implies
\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f},\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}\in L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon}),
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\leq 4|D_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\sup_{{\bf x}\in D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\sup_{\bar{{\bf x}}\in D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}\right),

and

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\\
\leq 4|D_{\varepsilon}|\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\sup_{{\bf x}\in D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right).

For the case
i=2
, as
\mathsf{f}\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
we get
\partial_{{\bf x}}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}\in H^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})\subset C^{\gamma}(D_{\varepsilon})
for
0<\gamma<1,
which give us

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\\
\hskip-14.22636pt\leq|D_{\varepsilon}|\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}|\bar{{\bf x}}|^{2\gamma}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right)\mbox{d}{\bf x}.

For the last case
i=3
, as
\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}\in H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})\subset C^{1}(D_{\varepsilon})
, we obtain

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\\
\leq|D_{\varepsilon}|\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}|\bar{{\bf x}}|^{2}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right)\mbox{d}{\bf x}.

Finally, for the sake of brevity and clarity we will refer to Lemma
7.4
, where the last term of each of the above expressions is bounded.
∎

4.2.
Hypothesis 1 and 2

On one hand, the hypothesis 1 in the C-R theorem is trivial to check. On the other hand, the hypothesis 2 has to do with the regularity of the functional
F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]
with respect to
\mathsf{f}
and
\lambda
.

We present the regularity of the functional in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

For all
0<\varepsilon<1,
there exist
0<\delta(\varepsilon)\ll 1
small enough such that the following holds true:

(1)

The functional
F:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))\rightarrow Y(D_{\varepsilon})
is of class
C^{1}.

(2)

The partial derivative
D^{2}_{\lambda,\mathsf{f}}F:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))\rightarrow Y(D_{\varepsilon})
is continuous.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition is rather standard. We include the main details here for sake of completeness.

The continuity of the functional is trivial for any derivative involving
D_{\lambda}
. Consequently, the result reduces to check that

D_{\mathsf{f}}F:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))\rightarrow Y(D_{\varepsilon})
is continuous at the origin.
This will be done by showing first the existence of the Gâteaux derivative and second its continuity in the strong topology.
A refined analysis concerning its connection with Fréchet derivative will be developed in the next section.
The Gâteaux differential of
F[\lambda,\cdot]
at
\mathsf{f}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
in the direction
h\in X(D_{\varepsilon})

is defined as

D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]h:=\frac{\mbox{d}}{{\mbox{d}\tau}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}+\tau h]|_{\tau=0}=\lim_{\tau\to 0}\frac{F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}+\tau h]-F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}.

A formal straightforward computation show that this derivative is given by

(27)

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]h({\bf x})=

\displaystyle\lambda\partial_{x}h({\bf x})+(y+\mathsf{f}({\bf x}))\partial_{x}h({\bf x})+h({\bf x})\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}({\bf x})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\varpi^{\prime}(y-\bar{y})K[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\varpi^{\prime}(y-\bar{y})(\partial_{x}K)[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}.

To prove this rigorously, we need to get

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\frac{F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}+\tau h]-F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]}{\tau}-D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]h\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

By virtue of (
27
) it is enough to prove that

(28)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\varpi^{\prime}(y-\bar{y})\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle=0,

(29)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\varpi^{\prime}(y-\bar{y})\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right)\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle=0,

where the new kernel is

(30)

\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}}):=K[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})-K[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})=\log\left[\frac{\cosh\left(\bar{y}+\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]({\bf x})\right)-\cos(\bar{x})}{\cosh\left(\bar{y}+\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]({\bf x})\right)-\cos(\bar{x})}\right],

and the auxiliary functions
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}
are given respectively by

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}[g]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}}):=\frac{\sinh(\bar{y}+\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x}))}{\cosh(\bar{y}+\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x}))-\cos(\bar{x})},

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}[g]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}}):=\frac{\cosh(\bar{y}+\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x}))}{\cosh(\bar{y}+\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[g]({\bf x}))-\cos(\bar{x})}.

In order to do the manuscript more readable, we redirect the reader to the next subsection for precise proofs of each of the above limits (
28
), (
29
). As we can imagine the computations are very long and tedious but share lot of similarities.

4.2.1.
Computation of (
28
)

As the profile
\varpi
is a smooth and compactly supported function, condition
(
28
)
reduces to check

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

Remark:
There are not boundary terms to handle due to the support of
\varpi
and definition of
D_{\varepsilon}(y).

Consequently, the above reduces to prove that each of the terms of
H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})-
norm tends to zero.
In the same spirit of (
25
), the proof of (
28
) reduces to check that

(31)

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{i}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3-i}\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\leq C(\varepsilon,\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})})\tau^{2}\qquad(0\leq i\leq 3).

To do that, we will use repeatedly inequality (
26
) and the fact that, without lost of generality, the direction
h\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
can be taken with norm
\|h\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}=1
.

For the case
i=0
or
i=1
, as
h\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
implies
\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\partial_{x}h,\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\partial_{x}h\in L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\leq 4|D_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\sup_{{\bf x}\in D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\sup_{\bar{{\bf x}}\in D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}\right),

and

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\\
\leq 4|D_{\varepsilon}|\sup_{{\bf x}\in D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right).

For the case
i=2
, as
h\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
we get
\partial_{{\bf x}}\partial_{x}h\in H^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})\subset C^{\gamma}(D_{\varepsilon})
for
0<\gamma<1,
which give us

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\\
\leq|D_{\varepsilon}|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}|\bar{{\bf x}}|^{2\gamma}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right)\mbox{d}{\bf x}.

For the last case
i=3
, as
\partial_{x}h\in H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})\subset C^{1}(D_{\varepsilon})
, we obtain

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\\
\leq|D_{\varepsilon}|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\right|^{2}|\bar{{\bf x}}|^{2}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right)\mbox{d}{\bf x}.

As
\mathsf{f}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
and our goal is to compute the limit as
\tau\to 0,
we can assume without loss of generality that
0<\tau\ll 1
is small enough such that
\mathsf{f}+\tau h\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
. Consequently, the last term of each of the above expressions can be handle applying auxiliary Lemma
7.5
with

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{f}+\tau h,

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{f}.

Finally, as by hypothesis
\|h\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}=1
, we get

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}\leq C(\varepsilon,\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})})\tau^{2},

and taking the limit as
\tau\to 0
we have proved (
28
) .

4.2.2.
Computation of (
29
)

Similarly, since the profile
\varpi
is a smooth and compactly supported function, condition
(
29
)
reduces to check

\lim_{\tau\to 0}\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right)\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}=0.

As before, there are not boundary terms to handle due to the support of
\varpi
and definition
D_{\varepsilon}(y).

Consequently, the above reduces to prove that each of the terms of
H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})-
norm tends to zero.
In the same spirit of (
25
) or (
28
), the proof of (
29
) reduces to check that

(32)

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{i}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right\}\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3-i}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\hskip-14.22636pt\leq C(\varepsilon,\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})})\tau^{2},

for
0\leq i\leq 3
and
\mathsf{f}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon}))
with
\delta(\varepsilon)
small enough and
h\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
with
\|h\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}=1.

Now proceeding as before, we use repeatedly inequality (
26
) and the fact that
\|h\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}=1
.

For the case
i=0
or
i=1
, as
\mathsf{f}\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
implies
\partial_{x}\mathsf{f},\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}\in L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon}),
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right)\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 4|D_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\sup_{{\bf x}\in D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\sup_{\bar{{\bf x}}\in D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right|^{2}\right),

and

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right\}\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 4|D_{\varepsilon}|\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\sup_{{\bf x}\in D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right\}\right|^{2}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right).

For the case
i=2
, as
\mathsf{f}\in H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})
we get
\partial_{{\bf x}}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}\in H^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})\subset C^{\gamma}(D_{\varepsilon})
for
0<\gamma<1,
which give us

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right\}\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{{\bf x}}\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq|D_{\varepsilon}|\|\mathsf{f}\|^{2}_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{2}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right\}\right|^{2}|\bar{{\bf x}}|^{2\gamma}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right)\mbox{d}{\bf x}.

For the last case
i=3
, as
\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}\in H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})\subset C^{1}(D_{\varepsilon})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right\}\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{L^{2}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq|D_{\varepsilon}|\|\mathsf{f}\|^{2}_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}\int_{D_{\varepsilon}}\left(\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left|\partial_{{\bf x}}^{3}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right\}\right|^{2}|\bar{{\bf x}}|^{2}\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right)\mbox{d}{\bf x}.

For the sake of clarity, the last term of each of the above expressions can be handle using auxiliary Lemma
7.6
.
After that, collecting all we get

\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\left(\frac{\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}+\tau h,\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})}{\tau}-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\right)\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}^{2}\leq C(\varepsilon,\|\mathsf{f}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})})\tau^{2},

and taking the limit as
\tau\to 0
we have proved (
29
).

This shows the existence of Gâteaux derivative and now we intend to prove the continuity of the map
\mathsf{f}\to D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}]

from
X(D_{\varepsilon})
to the space
\mathsf{L}(X(D_{\varepsilon}),Y(D_{\varepsilon}))
of all bounded linear operators from
X(D_{\varepsilon})
to
Y(D_{\varepsilon})
. This is a consequence of the following estimate:

(33)

\|D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]h-D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]h\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})},

for any pair
\mathsf{f}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(X(D_{\varepsilon}))
and
h\in X(D_{\varepsilon}).

As the profile function
\varpi
is smooth it is easy to check that condition (
33
) holds if and only if the following bounds are satisfied

(34)

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}\mathcal{K}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})},

(35)

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{D_{\varepsilon}(y)}(\partial_{x}K[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{x}K[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}])({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[h]({\bf x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}\right\|_{H^{3}(D_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}\|_{H^{4,3}(D_{\varepsilon})}.

Note that (
34
) follows by direct application of Lemma
7.5
. In order to obtain (
35
), we just need to note that adding and subtracting appropriate terms we have

(\partial_{x}K[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\partial_{x}K[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}])({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})=\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}-\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime})]({\bf x})+(\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime}]-\Psi_{1}[\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}])({\bf x},\bar{{\bf x}})\Delta_{\bar{{\bf x}}}[\partial_{x}\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}]({\bf x}).

The first term of the above expression is trivially bounded and for the other one we just need to use auxiliary Lemma
7.3
. Consequently, we have obtained that the Gâteaux derivatives are continuous
with respect to the strong topology and hence they are in fact Fréchet derivatives. Therefore, we can conclude that the Fréchet derivative exists and coincides with the Gâteaux derivative. See
[
28
]
for more details.
∎

5.
Analysis of the linear part

Hypothesis 3 and 4 in the C-R theorem have to do with the linear part of equation (
16
).

Recall that the linearization of (
22
) around
\mathsf{f}=0
, thanks to the expression (
27
), is given by

\mathcal{L}[\lambda]h:=D_{\mathsf{f}}F[\lambda,0]h.

That is,

(36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\lambda]h({\bf x})=\,

\displaystyle(\lambda+y)\partial_{x}h({\bf x})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times I_{\varepsilon}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})\log\left[\cosh(\bar{y}-\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x}-\bar{x})\right](\partial_{x}h({\bf x})-\partial_{x}h(\bar{{\bf x}}))\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}.

In order to study both the kernel and image of
\mathcal{L}[\lambda]
we will introduce some modification which allows us to realize an asymptotic analysis on
\varepsilon
.

5.1.
Decomposition of the linear operator

In first place, we define a primitive function
\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
of the profile function
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}

by

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y):=\int_{0}^{y}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{y}.

Then

(37)

\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times I_{\varepsilon}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})\log\left[\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right]\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}=\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y).

Proof of (
37
).

Since

\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\left[\cosh(\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x})\right]\mbox{d}\bar{x}=\frac{1}{2}\left(|\bar{y}|-\log 2\right),

we have that

(38)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}\times I_{\varepsilon}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})\log\left[\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right]\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|y-\bar{y}|\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{sgn}(y-\bar{y})\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})d\bar{y}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{sgn}(y-\bar{y})\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle=\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y)+\lim_{R\to 0}\left(\mathrm{sgn}(y-R)\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(R)-\mathrm{sgn}(y+R)\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(-R)\right).

Since
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
is even, their primitive
\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
is odd. Thus the limit in the last line of (
38
) is zero.
∎

Using (
37
) one learns that

(39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\lambda]h({\bf x})=\,

\displaystyle(\lambda+y-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))\partial_{x}h({\bf x})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times I_{\varepsilon}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})\log\left[\cosh(\bar{y}-\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x}-\bar{x})\right]\partial_{x}h(\bar{{\bf x}}))\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}.

Now, recalling that
h\in X(D_{\varepsilon})
, we can use the expansion

(40)

h({\bf x})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}(y)\cos(nx),

with

(41)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{3}\sum_{k=0}^{4-j}n^{2k}\|\partial^{j}_{y}h_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}<\infty,

which give us that

(42)

\mathcal{L}[\lambda]\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}(y)\cos(nx)\right)\\
=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)n\sin(nx)\left((\lambda+y-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h_{n}(y)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{I_{\varepsilon}}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})h_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y}\right).

Proof of (
42
).

We compute
\mathcal{L}[\lambda]
acting on the mode
h_{n}(y)\cos(nx)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\lambda](h_{n}(y)\cos(nx))=

\displaystyle-n\sin(nx)(\lambda+y-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h_{n}(y)

\displaystyle-\frac{n}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}\times I_{\varepsilon}}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})\log\left(\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right)h_{n}(\bar{y})\sin(n\bar{x})\mbox{d}\bar{{\bf x}}.

Now, we integrate in the
\bar{x}-
variable on the above double integral, which give us

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\left[\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(x-\bar{x})\right]\sin(n\bar{x})\mbox{d}\bar{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\left[\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x})\right]\sin(n(x-\bar{x}))\mbox{d}\bar{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(nx)}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\left[\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x})\right]\cos(n\bar{x})\mbox{d}\bar{x},

where the last step is due to trigonometric identities and the parity of the integrands.
In addition, last integral can be written as

\frac{\sin(nx)}{n}\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\left[\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x})\right](\sin(n\bar{x}))^{\prime}\mbox{d}\bar{x}\\
=-\frac{\sin(nx)}{n}\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\sin(\bar{x})\sin(n\bar{x})}{\cosh(y-\bar{y})-\cos(\bar{x})}\mbox{d}\bar{x}=-\frac{\sin(nx)}{n}\frac{1}{2}e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}.

This last integral can be computed, for example, by using residues. These last computations yield

\mathcal{L}[\lambda]\left(h_{n}(y)\cos(nx)\right)=-n\sin(nx)\left((\lambda+y-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h_{n}(y)-\frac{1}{2n}\int_{I_{\varepsilon}}\omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})h(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y}\right).

Combining everything we obtain that
\mathcal{L}[\lambda]h({\bf x})
admits an expansion given by

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)n\sin(nx)\left[(\lambda+y)h_{n}(y)-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y)h_{n}(y)-\frac{1}{2n}\int_{I_{\varepsilon}}\omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{y})h_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y}\right].

∎

To sum up, for any
h\in H^{3}(I_{\varepsilon})
the operators
\mathcal{L}_{n}[\lambda]
will be define as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}[\lambda]h(y):=-n(\lambda+y-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h(y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{I_{\varepsilon}}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})h(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y},

in such a way that, in
X(D_{\varepsilon})
, the full operator is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[\lambda]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sin(nx)\mathcal{L}_{n}[\lambda]\Pi_{n},

where
\Pi_{n}
is just the projector onto
\cos(nx)
.

5.2.
Rescaling of the decomposition

Since
I_{\varepsilon}=[-1-\varepsilon,-1+\varepsilon]\cup[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon],
we will make a reflection to consider just the domain
y\in[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon].
Recall that
\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
and
\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
are odd functions, then we have that

\mathcal{L}_{n}[\lambda]h_{n}(+y)\\
=-n(\lambda+y-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h_{n}(+y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})h_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y},\quad\text{on }y\in\left(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\right),

and

\mathcal{L}_{n}[\lambda]h_{n}(-y)=-n(\lambda-y+\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h_{n}(-y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})h_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y+\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y},\quad\text{on }y\in\left(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\right).

In addition, the integral part can be write as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})h_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y}=\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{1+\varepsilon}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\left(h_{n}(y)e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}-h_{n}(-y)e^{-n|y+\bar{y}|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{y},

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})h_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y+\bar{y}|}\mbox{d}\bar{y}=\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{1+\varepsilon}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\left(h_{n}(y)e^{-n|y+\bar{y}|}-h_{n}(-y)e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{y}.

Thus, if we define on
y\in\left(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\right)
the auxiliary functions

\begin{array}[]{r@{{}\mathrel{}{}}l}h^{+}_{n}(y)&:=h_{n}(+y),\\
\mathcal{L}_{n}^{+}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}h_{n}^{+}\\
h_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(y)&:=\mathcal{L}_{n}[\lambda]h_{n}(+y),\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{r@{{}\mathrel{}{}}l}h^{-}_{n}(y)&:=h_{n}(-y),\\
\mathcal{L}_{n}^{-}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}h_{n}^{+}\\
h_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(y)&:=\mathcal{L}_{n}[\lambda]h_{n}(-y),\end{array}

we find on
y\in\left(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon\right)
that

\mathcal{L}_{n}^{+}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}h_{n}^{+}\\
h_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(y)\\
=-n(\lambda+y-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h_{n}^{+}(y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{1+\varepsilon}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\left(h^{+}_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}-h^{-}_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y+\bar{y}|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{y},

and

\mathcal{L}_{n}^{-}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}h_{n}^{+}\\
h_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(y)\\
=-n(\lambda-y+\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y))h_{n}^{-}(y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{1+\varepsilon}\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\left(h^{+}_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y+\bar{y}|}-h^{-}_{n}(\bar{y})e^{-n|y-\bar{y}|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{y}.

Now, we make the change of variables
z=\frac{\bar{y}-1}{\varepsilon}
or
\bar{y}=1+\varepsilon z
inside of the integrals to get

\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\varepsilon\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)\left(h^{+}_{n}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)e^{-n|y-\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)|}-h^{-}_{n}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)e^{-n|y+\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)|}\right)\,\mbox{d}z,

and

\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\varepsilon\varpi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)\left(h^{+}_{n}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)e^{-n|y+\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)|}-h^{-}_{n}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)e^{-n|y-\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)|}\right)\,\mbox{d}z

We define on
z\in(-1,1)
the auxiliary functions

\begin{array}[]{r@{{}\mathrel{}{}}l}w_{n}^{+}(z)&:=h_{n}^{+}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right),\\
L^{+}_{n}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}w_{n}^{+}\\
w_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(z)&:=\mathcal{L}_{n}^{+}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}h_{n}^{+}\\
h_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(1+\varepsilon z),\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{r@{{}\mathrel{}{}}l}w_{n}^{-}(z)&:=h_{n}^{-}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right),\\
L^{-}_{n}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}w_{n}^{+}\\
w_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(z)&:=\mathcal{L}_{n}^{-}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}h_{n}^{+}\\
h_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)(1+\varepsilon z).\end{array}

Now, recalling that
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y)=\varepsilon\varphi_{\kappa}((y-1)/\varepsilon)
, we find that

\displaystyle L^{+}_{n}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}w_{n}^{+}\\
w_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)=

\displaystyle-n\left(\lambda+1+\varepsilon z-\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)\right)w_{n}^{+}(z)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\varepsilon\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\left(w^{+}_{n}(\bar{z})e^{-n\varepsilon|z-\bar{z}|}-w^{-}_{n}\left(\bar{z}\right)e^{-n\left|2+\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})\right|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{z},

and

\displaystyle L^{-}_{n}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}w_{n}^{+}\\
w_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)=

\displaystyle-n\left(\lambda-1-\varepsilon z+\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)\right)w_{n}^{-}(z)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\varepsilon\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})\left(w^{+}_{n}\left(\bar{z}\right)e^{-n|2+\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})|}-w^{-}_{n}\left(\bar{z}\right)e^{-n\varepsilon|z-\bar{z}|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{z}.

In addition, one can compute that

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(y)=\int_{0}^{y}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{y}=\int_{0}^{1-\varepsilon}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{y}+\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{y}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{y}=\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)+\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{y}\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(\bar{y})\mbox{d}\bar{y}.

Thus, the primitive can be write in a more convenient way as

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}(1+\varepsilon z)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)+\int_{-1}^{1+\varepsilon z}\varepsilon\varphi_{\kappa}\left(\frac{\bar{y}-1}{\varepsilon}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{y}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{-1}^{z}\varphi_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}=\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\left(-1+\int_{-1}^{z}\varphi_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}\right).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}\left(1+\varepsilon z\right)=\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\Phi_{\kappa}(z),

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{\kappa}(z):=-1+\int_{-1}^{z}\varphi_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}.

Combining all we have proved that

\displaystyle L^{+}_{n}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}w_{n}^{+}\\
w_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)=

\displaystyle-n\left(\lambda+1-\varepsilon+\varepsilon z-\varepsilon^{2}\Phi_{\kappa}(z)\right)w_{n}^{+}(z)

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\left(w^{+}_{n}(\bar{z})e^{-n\varepsilon|z-\bar{z}|}-w^{-}_{n}\left(\bar{z}\right)e^{-n\left|2+\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})\right|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{z},

\displaystyle L^{-}_{n}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}w_{n}^{+}\\
w_{n}^{-}\end{array}\right)=

\displaystyle-n\left(\lambda-1+\varepsilon-\varepsilon z+\varepsilon^{2}\Phi_{\kappa}(z)\right)w_{n}^{-}(z)

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})\left(w^{+}_{n}\left(\bar{z}\right)e^{-n|2+\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})|}-w^{-}_{n}\left(\bar{z}\right)e^{-n\varepsilon|z-\bar{z}|}\right)\mbox{d}\bar{z}.

5.3.
One dimensionality of the kernel of the linear operator

The following section consists on two well-differentiated parts. On one hand, we will prove that there exists an element in the kernel of the linear operator. On the other hand, we will prove that the kernel is the span of this element. To sum up, the main result of this section is to prove the following result:

Theorem 5.1
.

For any
M>1,
there exist positive
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(M)
and positive
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(M)
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
0\leq\kappa<\kappa_{0}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m<M
, we can find
\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}\in\mathbb{R}
and a
\frac{2\pi}{m}-
periodic and non-identically zero function
h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}\in X(D_{\varepsilon})
solving

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}[\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}]h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}=0,

where the functional
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}[\lambda]
is given in (
36
).
In addition, the kernel
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon,\kappa}[\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}]
on
X(D_{\varepsilon})
is the span of
h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}
and the regularity of the solution is in fact
h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}\in C^{\infty}(D_{\varepsilon}
).

Importantly,
h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}(x,y)
, with
x\in\mathbb{T}
and
y\in I_{\varepsilon}
, depends non trivially on
x
.

Remark 5.2
.

In the statement of the theorem it has been made explicit the dependence of
\mathcal{L}[\lambda]
on the parameters
\varepsilon
and
\kappa
although it has not been in (
36
). It is easy to check that
\mathcal{L}[\lambda]
in (
36
) depends on
\varepsilon
and
\kappa
through
\Omega_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
and
\varpi_{\varepsilon,\kappa}
.

Remark 5.3
.

It is important to emphasize that parameter
\varepsilon
does not depend on
\kappa
.

Remark 5.4
.

The speed of the traveling wave
\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}
satisfies the expansion in terms of
\varepsilon
-parameter:

(47)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}=1+\lambda^{1}_{\kappa,m}\varepsilon+\lambda^{2}_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}\varepsilon^{2},

where

\displaystyle\lambda^{1}_{\kappa,m}=O(1),

and

\displaystyle|\lambda^{2}_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}|\leq C(M).

Remark 5.5
.

In the rest of the section
5
we avoid to use subscripts
\varepsilon,\kappa,m
in expression (
47
), which we will ignore in favor of readability. We write with abuse of notation
\lambda=1+\lambda_{1}\varepsilon+\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{2}.

Remark 5.6
.

For the degenerate case
(\kappa=0)
, the same result works line by line for all
m\in\mathbb{N}.
That is, all the computations can be taken independent of
M.

5.3.1.
Proof of existence

In order to show the existence of
(\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m},h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m})
solving

\mathcal{L}[\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}]h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}=0,

we fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m<M
, and take

\displaystyle w^{+}_{n}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&n\neq m,\\
a(z)&n=m,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle w^{-}_{n}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&n\neq m,\\
b(z)&n=m,\end{array}\right.

where
a
and
b
depend on
\varepsilon,\kappa
and
m
but we do not make this dependence explicit for sake of simplicity. Thus, we have to find
(\lambda,a,b)\in\mathbb{R}\times H^{3}([-1,1]\times H^{3}([-1,1])
solving

\displaystyle L^{+}_{m}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a\\
b\end{array}\right)=0,

\displaystyle L^{-}_{m}[\lambda]\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a\\
b\end{array}\right)=0,

where
L^{\pm}_{m}
are given in (
5.2
) and (
5.2
). After that, the solution
(\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m},\,h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m})
will be given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}=\lambda,

\displaystyle h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}(x,y)=a\left(\frac{y-1}{\varepsilon}\right)\cos(mx)\quad\text{for $y\in[+1-\varepsilon,+1+\varepsilon]$},

\displaystyle h_{\varepsilon,\kappa,m}(x,y)=b\left(\frac{-y-1}{\varepsilon}\right)\cos(mx)\quad\text{for $y\in[-1-\varepsilon,-1+\varepsilon]$}.

In order to solve (
5.3.1
) and (
5.3.1
) we introduce the ansatzs

\displaystyle a(z)

\displaystyle=\hskip 35.56593pta_{1}(z)\varepsilon+a_{2}^{\varepsilon}(z)\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle b(z)

\displaystyle=b_{0}(z)+b^{\varepsilon}_{1}(z)\varepsilon,

and

\lambda=1+\lambda_{1}\varepsilon+\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{2},

where
a_{1}
,
b_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}
will depend on
\kappa
and
m
but they will not depend on
\varepsilon
. In addition, the remaining terms
a^{\varepsilon}_{2}
,
b^{\varepsilon}_{1})
and
\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{2}
depend on
\varepsilon
,
\kappa
and
m
.
Imposing the equations

(56)

\displaystyle(1+\lambda_{1}-z)b_{0}(z)=-\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})b_{0}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z},

(57)

\displaystyle 2a_{1}=-\frac{1}{2m}e^{-2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})b_{0}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z},

we get for
(\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon},a^{\varepsilon}_{2},b^{\varepsilon}_{1})
the system

(58)

\displaystyle 2a^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z)+\frac{e^{-2m}}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})b^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\bar{z})e^{-m\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})}\mbox{d}\bar{z}=A_{0}[a_{1},b_{0}](z)+\varepsilon A_{1}[a^{\varepsilon}_{2},\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon};a_{1},b_{0},\lambda_{1}](z),

(59)

\displaystyle(1+\lambda_{1}-z)b_{1}^{\varepsilon}(z)+\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})b_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\bar{z})\mbox{d}\bar{z}+\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{2}b_{0}(z)=B_{0}[a_{1},b_{0}](z)+\varepsilon B_{1}[a_{2}^{\varepsilon},b_{1}^{\varepsilon},\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{2}](z),

where

\displaystyle A_{0}[a_{1},\,b_{0}](z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})a_{1}(\bar{z})e^{-m\varepsilon|z-\bar{z}|}\mbox{d}\bar{z}-\frac{e^{-2m}}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})b_{0}(\bar{z})\frac{e^{-m\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})}-1}{\varepsilon}\mbox{d}\bar{z}+\varepsilon a_{1}\Phi_{\kappa}(z),

\displaystyle B_{0}[a_{1},\,b_{0}](z)

\displaystyle=-\Phi_{\kappa}(z)b_{0}(z)+\frac{e^{-2m}}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})a_{1}(\bar{z})e^{-m\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})}\mbox{d}\bar{z},

and

\displaystyle A_{1}[a^{\varepsilon}_{2},\,\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon};\,a_{1},\,\lambda_{1}](z)=-

\displaystyle(-1+\lambda_{1}+z)a^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z)-a_{1}\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon(\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{2}-\Phi_{\kappa}(z))a^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})a^{\varepsilon}_{2}(\bar{z})e^{-m\varepsilon|z-\bar{z}|}\mbox{d}\bar{z},

\displaystyle B_{1}[a^{\varepsilon}_{2},\,b_{1}^{\varepsilon},\,\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}](z)=-

\displaystyle(\Phi_{\kappa}(z)+\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon})b^{\varepsilon}_{1}(z)+\frac{e^{-2m}}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})a_{2}^{\varepsilon}(\bar{z})e^{-m\varepsilon(z+\bar{z})}\mbox{d}\bar{z}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})b_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\bar{z})\frac{e^{-m\varepsilon|z-\bar{z}|}-1}{\varepsilon}\mbox{d}\bar{z}.

Here, we note that

\displaystyle\|A_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(|a_{1}|+\|b_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\|A_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(1+|\lambda_{1}|\right)\|a_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+C|a_{1}||\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{2}|+C|\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{2}|\|a_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+C(1+\varepsilon)\|a_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle\|B_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle C\left(|a_{1}|+\|b_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\|B_{1}\|_{L^{2}}\leq

\displaystyle C\|b_{1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+C\|b_{1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}|\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}|+C\left(\|a_{2}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}+\|b_{1}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\right),

where the constant
C
does not depend on neither
\varepsilon
,
\kappa
or
m
, and
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}
.

So first we will solve (
56
) on
b_{0}
by choosing
\lambda_{1}
in a suitable way. This will give
a_{1}
in (
57
). Then, equations (
58
) and (
59
) will be written to be able to determine
a_{2}^{\varepsilon}
,
b_{1}^{\varepsilon}
and
\lambda_{2}^{\varepsilon}
by a contraction argument in
\varepsilon
.

We deal with equation (
56
) in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.7
.

For any
M>1,
there exists
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(M)
such that for all
1\leq m<M
and
0\leq\kappa<\kappa_{0}
, we can find a solution
(\lambda_{1},\,b_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times C^{\infty}([-1,1])
of equation (
56
). In addition, fixed
m
and
\kappa
, this solution is unique (modulo multiplication by constant) and satisfies

(60)

\displaystyle b_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{1}-z},

(61)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=\frac{2}{e^{4m}-1}+\emph{error},

where

\displaystyle|\emph{error}|\leq C(M)\kappa.

Moreover, we get

\displaystyle\|b_{0}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}\leq C(M),

\displaystyle\|\partial_{z}^{k)}b_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,\,1])}\leq C(k,M),\quad k=0,1,2,...

\displaystyle a_{1}=\frac{1}{2}e^{-2m}.

Remark 5.8
.

It is important to emphasize that constant
C(M)
depend on
M
but it does not depend on neither
m
or
\kappa
, for
0\leq\kappa<\kappa_{0}
and
1\leq m<M
.

Before prove the previous result, we pick
\lambda_{1}
appropriately. Notice that from (
56
) we see that
b_{0}
must be of the form

(62)

\displaystyle b_{0}(z)=\frac{C}{1+\lambda_{1}-z},

for some free constant
C
. Plugging (
62
) into (
56
) one finds that

\displaystyle 1=\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{-\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})}{1+\lambda_{1}-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z},

which is an equation for
\lambda_{1}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any
M>1,
there exist
\kappa^{\prime}_{0}=\kappa^{\prime}_{0}(M)
such that for all
0\leq\kappa\leq\kappa^{\prime}_{0}
and
1\leq m<M
, we can find
\lambda_{1}>0
(depending on
\kappa
and
m
) such that

(63)

\displaystyle 1=\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{-\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})}{1+\lambda_{1}-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}.

In addition, fixed
m
and
\kappa
this solution is unique and satisfies

\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda^{*}(M)>0,

where
\lambda^{*}(M)
just depends on
M
(it does not depend on either
\kappa
or
m
).

Proof.

We will use the properties of
\varphi_{\kappa}
stated in Lemma
2.2
. Firstly, we define the function

\displaystyle I(\lambda):=\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{-\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})}{1+\lambda-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z},

for
\lambda\geq 0
. We have that
I(\lambda)
is positive, continuous, decreasing with
I^{\prime}(\lambda)<0
and satisfying

(64)

\lim_{\lambda\to+\infty}I(\lambda)=0.

In addition, using Lemma
2.2
we obtain the following lower bound:

\displaystyle I(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{-\varphi_{\kappa}^{\prime}(\bar{z})}{1+\lambda-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}\geq\frac{1}{2M}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{-\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})}{1+\lambda-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}\geq\frac{1}{2M}\int_{-1}^{1-\kappa}\frac{-\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})}{1+\lambda-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2M}\int_{-1}^{1-\kappa}\frac{-\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-\tfrac{1}{2}}{1+\lambda-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}+\frac{1}{4M}\int_{-1}^{1-\kappa}\frac{1}{1+\lambda-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2M}\frac{1}{\lambda+\kappa}\|\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|_{L^{1}([-1,1-\kappa])}+\frac{1}{4M}\left(\log(2+\lambda)-\log(\lambda+\kappa)\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{C}{2M}\frac{\kappa}{\lambda+\kappa}+\frac{1}{4M}\log(2+\lambda)-\frac{1}{4M}\log(\lambda+\kappa),

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{C}{2M}-\frac{1}{4M}\log(\lambda+\kappa).

Let
\gamma
be small enough such that

1\leq-\frac{C}{2M}-\frac{1}{4M}\log(\gamma).

Notice that
\gamma
only depends on
M
. Then, taking
\kappa^{\prime}_{0}=\gamma/2
and
\lambda^{*}=\gamma/2
, we have proved that

\displaystyle I(\lambda^{*})\geq-\frac{C}{2M}-\frac{1}{4M}\log(\gamma/2+\kappa)\geq-\frac{C}{2M}-\frac{1}{4M}\log(\gamma/2+\kappa^{\prime}_{0})\geq 1.

for any
0\leq\kappa\leq\kappa^{\prime}_{0}=\gamma/2
and
1\leq m\leq M
.

Combining the lower bound with (
64
) and the fact that
I(\lambda)
is strictly decreasing, we can conclude that there exists a unique
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda^{*}
solving (
63
) for all
0\leq\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}_{0}
and
1\leq m\leq M
.
∎

After choose a suitable
\lambda_{1}>\lambda^{*}(M)
by Lemma
5.9
, we obtain
b_{0}
through equation (
62
). Now, we have all the ingredients to check the conclusions of Proposition
5.7
.

Proof of Proposition
5.7
.

Notice that

b_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{1}-z},

solves (
56
) and

\|b_{0}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{(2+\lambda_{1})\lambda_{1}}}<\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}(M)}}
, with
\lambda^{*}(M)
given by Lemma
5.9
. We also have that
\|\partial_{z}^{k)}b_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}\leq C(k,M)
. From (
57
) and (
63
) we have that
a_{1}=\frac{1}{2}e^{-2m}
.

In order to get (
61
) we proceed as follow. From the identity (
63
) we get

(65)

\displaystyle 1=\frac{1}{2m}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{-\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1/2}{1+\lambda_{1}-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}+\frac{1}{4m}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{1}-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}=\frac{1}{4m}\left(\Lambda_{1}+\log\left(\frac{2+\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{1}}\right)\right),

where

\Lambda_{1}:=2\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{-\varphi^{\prime}_{\kappa}(\bar{z})-1/2}{1+\lambda_{1}-\bar{z}}\mbox{d}\bar{z}.

Solving (
65
) we obtain that

\lambda_{1}=\frac{2}{e^{4m-\Lambda_{1}}-1},

and consequently
